=== PROMPT VARIANT ===
r1

=== PROMPT ===
You will be given a competitive programming problem.
In the nation of Atcoder, there are N cities numbered 1 to N, and M trains numbered 1 to M.
Train i departs from city A_i at time S_i and arrives at city B_i at time T_i.
Given a positive integer X_1, find a way to set non-negative integers X_2,\ldots,X_M that satisfies the following condition with the minimum possible value of X_2+\ldots+X_M.

- Condition: For all pairs (i,j) satisfying 1 \leq i,j \leq M, if B_i=A_j and T_i \leq S_j, then T_i+X_i \leq S_j+X_j.
- In other words, for any pair of trains that are originally possible to transfer between, it is still possible to transfer even after delaying the departure and arrival times of each train i by X_i.



It can be proved that such a way to set X_2,\ldots,X_M with the minimum possible value of X_2+\ldots+X_M is unique.

Input

The input is given from Standard Input in the following format:
N M X_1
A_1 B_1 S_1 T_1
\vdots
A_M B_M S_M T_M

Output

Print X_2,\ldots,X_M that satisfy the condition with the minimum possible sum, in that order, separated by spaces.

Constraints


- 2 \leq N \leq 2\times 10^5
- 2 \leq M \leq 2\times 10^5
- 1 \leq A_i,B_i \leq N
- A_i \neq B_i
- 0 \leq S_i < T_i \leq 10^9
- 1 \leq X_1 \leq 10^9
- All input values are integers.

Sample Input 1

3 6 15
1 2 10 20
1 2 20 30
2 3 25 40
2 3 35 50
3 1 15 30
3 1 45 60

Sample Output 1

0 10 0 0 5

The arrival of train 1 from city 1 to 2 is delayed by 15 and becomes time 35.
To allow transfer from train 1 to 3 in city 2, the departure of train 3 is delayed by 10, making it depart at time 35 and arrive at time 50.
Further, to allow transfer from train 3 to 6 in city 3, the departure of train 6 is delayed by 5, making it depart at time 50.
Other trains can operate without delay while still allowing transfers between originally transferable trains, so (X_2,X_3,X_4,X_5,X_6)=(0,10,0,0,5) satisfies the condition.
Moreover, there is no solution with a smaller sum that satisfies the condition, so this is the answer.

Sample Input 2

10 9 100
1 10 0 1
10 2 1 100
10 3 1 100
10 4 1 100
10 5 1 100
10 6 1 100
10 7 1 100
10 8 1 100
10 9 1 100

Sample Output 2

100 100 100 100 100 100 100 100

Sample Input 3

4 4 10
1 2 0 1
1 2 0 10
2 3 100 200
2 4 100 200

Sample Output 3

0 0 0

Reason in a commit-implement-recheck way.

A productive rhythm for this task usually looks like this:
- Isolate the decisive constraints, the target complexity, and at most one or two approaches that are still truly plausible.
- If two candidates remain, compare them only on the decisive constraint and commit quickly to the stronger one instead of keeping a wide search open.
- Before coding, keep only a tiny implementation ledger for the chosen plan: main invariant, key state or transition, and the one or two edge cases most likely to fail.
- Move into code as soon as that ledger is clear. After coding starts, keep reasoning attached to the current draft rather than reopening the high-level search.
- After the first complete draft, do one short implementation-check-adjust-check loop on the same draft: test the key edge case, patch locally if needed, and recheck.
- Reopen the main design only if a concrete counterexample breaks the core invariant. Otherwise prefer the local patch and keep closing.
- Once the current draft, invariant, and short recheck still agree, finalize the solution.

Try to avoid patterns like these:
- spending too long comparing nearby plans before committing to one workable approach;
- returning from code to broad algorithm discussion without a concrete contradiction;
- turning one local doubt into a full restart when the current draft only needs a small patch.

Output only the final Python code enclosed in a code block.

Now, let's think about segment tree implementation details.

We need range update [l,r] inclusive with value p (could be negative). Point query pos returns min p.

Simplest recursive:
class SegTree:
    def __init__(self,n):
        self.n=n
        self.lazy=[INF]*(4*n)
    def update(self, node, nl, nr, ql, qr, val):
        if qr < nl or nr < ql: return
        if ql <= nl and nr <= qr:
            if val < self.lazy[node]: self.lazy[node]=val
            return
        mid=(nl+nr)//2
        self.update(node*2, nl, mid, ql, qr, val)
        self.update(node*2+1, mid+1, nr, ql, qr, val)
    def query(self, node, nl, nr, pos):
        res=self.lazy[node]
        if nl==nr: return res
        mid=(nl+nr)//2
        if pos<=mid:
            child=self.query(node*2, nl, mid, pos)
        else:
            child=self.query(node*2+1, mid+1, nr, pos)
        if child < res: res=child
        return res
But recursion depth O(log M) ~18, fine. Number of updates M, each update O(log M) but recursive may visit O(log M) nodes for range update? Standard range update with lazy no push visits O(log n) nodes? Actually range update with full cover can stop; if partial, recurses. It visits O(log n + k) where k number of covered segments? For arbitrary range, O(log n) nodes? In segment tree, range update visits O(log n) nodes if no push? Actually range query/update visits O(log n + output nodes) where output nodes O(log n) for contiguous range? It can be O(log n) nodes? Standard range update visits O(log n) nodes? Let's recall: range update with lazy visits O(log n) nodes for interval? It decomposes interval into O(log n) canonical nodes. Yes O(log n). Good.

But Python recursion for 2e5 updates * ~log 18 = 3.6M calls, okay maybe but recursive function overhead okay? Need optimize maybe iterative segment tree? Could be fine in PyPy? 2e5 updates + 2e5 queries, each recursive ~O(log n) ~18, ~7M calls, okay maybe borderline but acceptable in Python if optimized. Need be careful.

We can implement iterative lazy segment tree for range chmin point query more efficiently. Since operation is range update min, point query min. There is a simple iterative segment tree using difference? Let's explore.

We need range chmin update and point query. This is equivalent to adding a "tag" over interval. We can use a segment tree with lazy tags stored at nodes, and point query traverses leaf to root. Iterative:
- n power of two? size.
- lazy = [INF]*(2*size)
- range_update(l,r,val): l += size; r += size; while l<=r: if l%2==1: lazy[l]=min(lazy[l],val); l+=1; if r%2==0: lazy[r]=min(lazy[r],val); r-=1; l//=2; r//=2
- point_query(pos): pos += size; res=INF; while pos>0: res=min(res,lazy[pos]); pos//=2; return res
This is standard iterative segment tree for range update point query. But careful: If we store tags at internal nodes, point query must traverse from leaf to root and take min. The range_update decomposes interval into nodes and updates lazy[node] with min. This works because any point in interval is covered by exactly one of the updated nodes? In iterative segment tree range update, the set of nodes updated forms a partition of the interval. Point query path from leaf to root includes the ancestor that is one of those updated nodes? Yes, for each updated node, all leaves in its subtree have that node as ancestor. So point query taking min along root-to-leaf path will include it. This is simpler and faster. Need set size as power of two >= M. lazy length 2*size. Initialize INF.

Let's test range_update iterative with inclusive l,r:
def range_update(l,r,val):
    l += size; r += size
    while l <= r:
        if l & 1:
            if val < lazy[l]: lazy[l] = val
            l += 1
        if not (r & 1):
            if val < lazy[r]: lazy[r] = val
            r -= 1
        l >>= 1; r >>= 1
This updates canonical nodes. Point query:
pos += size
res = INF
while pos:
    if lazy[pos] < res: res = lazy[pos]
    pos >>= 1
return res
Yes.

This is efficient.

Original edge constraint from j to k: x_k >= x_j + T_j - S_k. In terms of dist, x_j = max(0, X1 - dist[j]). Now, segment tree iterative:
size=1
while size < M: size <<=1
lazy=[INF]*(2*size)
def range_update(l,r,val):
    if l>r: return
    l += size; r += size
    while l <= r:
        if l & 1:
            if val < lazy[l]: lazy[l] = val
            l += 1
        if not (r & 1):
            if val < lazy[r]: lazy[r] = val
            r -= 1
        l >>= 1; r >>= 1
def point_query(p):
    p += size
    res = INF
    while p:
        v = lazy[p]
        if v < res: res = v
        p >>= 1
    return res

Need be careful with range_update when l and r are inclusive and after processing l/r, then shift. Standard.

Now, let's think about the proof that shortest path cost dist is exactly minimal waiting time. We process by T because graph DAG. Edge cost is S_j - T_i. This is the time between arrival of i and departure of j. If path source->...->j, dist is sum of waiting times. Then X1 - dist is the "slack" of delay? Let's derive original x formula with an example where path has multiple waits:
Train1 S0 T10, train2 S15 T20, train3 S25 T30. X1=10.
dist2=5, x2=5. dist3=dist2 + (25-20)=10, x3=0. Condition: train1 arrives20? Actually x1=10, arrival20. train2 dep25? original S15+x2=20? Wait original S2=15, x2=5 => dep20, arrival25. train3 dep25, x3=0 => dep25. Transfer from train2 to train3: T2+x2=25, S3+x3=25 okay. x3 = X1 - dist3 = 0. Good.
If X1=20, x2=15, train2 arrival35, train3 needs x3=10? dist3=10, x3=10. Condition train2 arrival35, train3 dep35. Good.

Now, let's consider if there is a path with multiple branches; x_i = max(0, X1 - dist_i) where dist_i is shortest waiting cost. Is it always the minimal vector? We argued yes. Let's test a small system with two paths to same node:
Train1 source -> A cost 5, -> B cost 10, both A/B -> C cost 0. dist_C=5 via A. x_C = X1-5. Constraint from B: x_C >= x_B + T_B - S_C. If x_B = X1-10, RHS = X1-10 + wait (0) = X1-10 <= X1-5, okay. Good.
If B has larger T_B? Wait cost to B = S_B - T_source. If cost 10, x_B = X1-10. Edge cost B->C = S_C - T_B. dist_C via B = 10 + S_C - T_B. The shortest dist_C is min. The constraint from B requires x_C >= X1 - dist_C_via_B? Actually x_C >= x_B + T_B - S_C = X1 - (dist_B + S_C - T_B) = X1 - dist_via_B. Since dist_C <= dist_via_B, X1 - dist_C >= X1 - dist_via_B, so satisfied. Good.

Now, let's think about nonnegativity and source fixed. Suppose dist_i > X1, x_i=0. But if there is an edge i->j with cost negative? No cost nonnegative, so dist_j >= dist_i > X1, x_j=0. Thus delays don't propagate from zero-delay trains. Good.

Now, could there be a constraint from train i with x_i=0 to train j that forces x_j >0 because T_i - S_j >0? But edge exists only if T_i <= S_j, so T_i - S_j <=0. Thus no. So zero-delay trains never force positive delay on successors. This is why x_i = max(0, X1 - dist_i) works. If edge condition didn't require T_i <= S_j, could be different. Good.

Now, let's consider if X1 is positive but dist_i = X1 exactly, x_i=0. Then edge to j with wait 0 gives dist_j = X1, x_j=0. Constraint T_i+x_i = T_i, S_j+x_j = S_j = T_i, okay. If wait >0, dist_j > X1, x_j=0, constraint T_i <= S_j, okay. Good.

Now, let's think about the uniqueness of minimal solution. Our x vector is componentwise minimal, so sum minimal. Good.

Now, let's see if there is any issue with using T_i as topological order when T_i can be equal and there is an edge with T_i <= S_j and S_j < T_j, so T_i < T_j. If T_i = T_j, impossible because S_j < T_j = T_i, so T_i <= S_j false. So no edge. Good.

Now, let's evaluate time complexity:
- Sorting city lists: total O(M log M) worst if one city, O(M log M). Sorting order O(M log M). Processing M trains, each point_query O(log M), each finite dist update binary search O(log len) + range_update O(log M). In worst all finite, ~ M*(logM + logM + logM) ~ 2e5*54 = 10.8M operations, plus segment tree loops. Python okay maybe 2-3 sec? Need optimize.

Potential performance bottlenecks:
- Recursive? We use iterative.
- Binary search per update: O(log len) in Python while loop. 2e5*18 = 3.6M iterations, okay.
- range_update iterative while loop O(log M) ~18 iterations, 3.6M.
- point_query O(log M) ~18, 3.6M.
Total ~10M simple operations, okay.
- Sorting city lists: if one city 2e5 sort O(M log M) ~3.6M comparisons with key calls, okay.
- Sorting order by T O(M log M).

Now, let's verify the formula x_i = max(0, X1 - dist[i]) with a case where dist[i] is not the shortest waiting cost but the longest path value? Let's derive again carefully to avoid sign error.

Original constraint: T_i + X_i <= S_j + X_j.
Let X_i = max(0, X1 - d_i) where d_i is some value. Then constraint becomes T_i + max(0, X1-d_i) <= S_j + max(0, X1-d_j).
If d_i <= X1 (so X_i = X1-d_i) and d_j <= X1 (X_j = X1-d_j), then T_i + X1 - d_i <= S_j + X1 - d_j => d_j <= d_i + S_j - T_i. So d_j should be <= d_i + wait. For shortest path, d_j = min over predecessors (d_i + wait). Then X_j = X1 - d_j is largest? Wait if d_j is smaller, X_j is larger. Constraint requires d_j <= d_i + wait. If we set d_j to shortest distance (minimum), then X_j is maximum, which seems opposite? Let's check.

We want minimal X_j satisfying lower bounds. If d_j = dist (minimum waiting cost), then X_j = X1 - dist. Since dist is small, X_j is large. That seems like maximal delay, not minimal. Wait sample1: dist3=5, X1=15, X3=10. Constraint from train1: X3 >= X1 + T1 - S3 =15+20-25=10. So minimal X3 is 10. dist3=5 gives X3=10. If dist were larger, X3 smaller, but would violate. So to satisfy X_j >= X1 - (dist_i+wait) for all paths, we need X_j >= X1 - min_path_cost? Let's derive.

For path P from source to j with total wait W = sum(S_next - T_current), the constraint chain implies X_j >= X1 + sum(T_current - S_next) = X1 - W. To satisfy all paths, X_j >= max_P (X1 - W_P) = X1 - min_P W_P. So minimal X_j is X1 - minW if positive. Thus dist = minW, X_j = X1 - dist. This is large when dist small. Yes, minimal delay is large? Wait if waiting times are small, delays propagate almost fully, so X_j large. If waiting times are large, the delay is absorbed by slack, so X_j small. So dist small -> X_j large. Correct. In sample, wait from train1 to train3 is 5, so delay reduces by 5: X3=10. Good.

Now, if dist_j = 0 (zero wait chain), X_j = X1, full delay propagates. If dist_j large >= X1, X_j=0. Good.

So formula correct.

Now, let's think about the DP for dist as shortest path. We update dist[j] = min(dist[j], dist[i] + S_j - T_i). This computes min waiting. Good.

Now, let's think about possible bug in iterative segment tree range_update when l and r are large and l/r cross. Standard code:
def range_update(l, r, val):
    l += size
    r += size
    while l <= r:
        if l & 1:
            lazy[l] = min(lazy[l], val)
            l += 1
        if not (r & 1):
            lazy[r] = min(lazy[r], val)
            r -= 1
        l >>= 1
        r >>= 1
Need ensure after l increment, if l > r, we still check r? In while condition, after processing l, we process r if r even. If l becomes > r, but r still maybe processed? Let's test l=5,r=5: l odd update node5 l=6; r odd, not (r&1) false; shift l=3,r=2; loop ends. Good. l=4,r=5: l even no; r odd no; shift l=2,r=2; l even no; r even update node2 r=1; shift l=1,r=0; end. Good. l=5,r=6: l odd update node5 l=6; r even update node6 r=5; shift l=3,r=2; end. Good. Standard.

Point query:
def point_query(p):
    p += size
    res = INF
    while p:
        if lazy[p] < res: res = lazy[p]
        p >>= 1
    return res
This includes leaf and all ancestors. Good.

Now, let's think about if range_update with val > existing lazy doesn't update. Since we want min, if val >= lazy[node], no need. But if val is larger than existing tag at a node, could it still be needed for some subrange because existing tag might not cover all leaves in node? Wait, lazy[node] stores a tag that applies to the entire node interval. If we update a subrange of this node with a larger val, and lazy[node] already has a smaller val covering the entire node, then the larger val is redundant for all leaves in the subrange because the smaller val already applies to the entire node. So min skip is safe. If lazy[node] has a smaller val from a previous update that covered the entire node, yes it covers all leaves. If lazy[node] has a smaller val from a previous update that covered the entire node? In our range_update, we only set lazy[node] when update range fully covers node interval. So lazy[node] always applies to entire node interval. Thus min skip safe. Good.

Now, let's think about if we need to push lazy tags for point query? No, because tags are persistent and min along path. If an update covers a node, it applies to all descendants; no need to push. If a later update covers a subrange, it sets a tag on a descendant. Point query takes min of both. Good.

</think>

=== ANSWER ===
with code.</think>```python
import sys

def main():
    input_data = list(map(int, sys.stdin.buffer.read().split()))
    it = iter(input_data)
    N = next(it)
    M = next(it)
    X1 = next(it)

    A = [0] * M
    B = [0] * M
    S = [0] * M
    T = [0] * M

    city_departures = [[] for _ in range(N)]

    for i in range(M):
        a = next(it) - 1
        b = next(it) - 1
        s = next(it)
        t = next(it)
        A[i] = a
        B[i] = b
        S[i] = s
        T[i] = t
        city_departures[a].append(i)

    # Sort trains departing from each city by departure time.
    for c in range(N):
        if len(city_departures[c]) > 1:
            city_departures[c].sort(key=S.__getitem__)

    city_start = [0] * N
    city_len = [0] * N
    cur = 0
    for c in range(N):
        city_start[c] = cur
        city_len[c] = len(city_departures[c])
        cur += city_len[c]

    pos = [0] * M
    for c in range(N):
        base = city_start[c]
        for k, idx in enumerate(city_departures[c]):
            pos[idx] = base + k

    INF = 10 ** 30

    size = 1
    while size < M:
        size <<= 1
    lazy = [INF] * (2 * size)

    def range_update(l, r, val):
        if l > r:
            return
        l += size
        r += size
        while l <= r:
            if l & 1:
                if val < lazy[l]:
                    lazy[l] = val
                l += 1
            if not (r & 1):
                if val < lazy[r]:
                    lazy[r] = val
                r -= 1
            l >>= 1
            r >>= 1

    def point_query(p):
        p += size
        res = INF
        while p:
            if lazy[p] < res:
                res = lazy[p]
            p >>= 1
        return res

    dist = [INF] * M
    order = sorted(range(M), key=T.__getitem__)

    for idx in order:
        if idx == 0:
            dist[idx] = 0
        else:
            p = point_query(pos[idx])
            if p < INF // 2:
                dist[idx] = S[idx] + p

        if dist[idx] < INF // 2:
            pval = dist[idx] - T[idx]
            c = B[idx]
            lst = city_departures[c]

            # First train in this city with S >= T[idx]
            lo = 0
            hi = len(lst)
            ti = T[idx]
            while lo < hi:
                mid = (lo + hi) // 2
                if S[lst[mid]] < ti:
                    lo = mid + 1
                else:
                    hi = mid

            l = city_start[c] + lo
            r = city_start[c] + city_len[c] - 1
            if l <= r:
                range_update(l, r, pval)

    ans = []
    for i in range(1, M):
        d = dist[i]
        if d >= INF // 2:
            ans.append("0")
        else:
            v = X1 - d
            if v < 0:
                v = 0
            ans.append(str(v))

    sys.stdout.write(" ".join(ans))

if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()
```
